Very curious and quite rare. — One can
look to this book for new evidence
(after so many others!) of the Templar origins
of Masonry<gloss>*</gloss>; do not
forget the publication date of the present
work: 1786. It is prior to the French
Revolution, the events of which
almost all bear the double stamp
of both the Masons and the Templars. One cannot, therefore,
say that the "Templar fable" was imag-
ined after the fact — See, moreover, the decisive
confessions of Cagliostro <note>Count Alessandro di Cagliostro (1743–1795), a famous occultist and healer involved in the Affair of the Diamond Necklace</note> before the Inquisi-
tion of Rome (*Life & Trial of Joseph Balsamo*,
octavo <note>Original "in-8º": a book size where each sheet is folded into 8 leaves</note>, 1790 for the Italian edition, and 1791 for the
French). Compare these revelations with
those of Barruel <note>Abbé Augustin Barruel, who famously proposed a conspiracy theory involving Masons in the French Revolution</note>, of Cadet-Gassicourt <note>Charles-Louis Cadet de Gassicourt, an author who claimed the Revolution was a Templar revenge plot</note>, etc.

<meta>This is the title page of a 1786 volume. It sets the stage for a critical examination of various esoteric, mystical, and pseudo-scientific movements that were highly influential during the late Enlightenment period.</meta>

# OBSERVATIONS

### ON

## <term>FREEMASONRY</term>,
<note>By the late 1700s, Freemasonry had become a pervasive social and intellectual force in Europe, offering a space for ritual, networking, and the discussion of Enlightenment ideals outside of traditional Church and State structures.</note>

## <term>MARTINISM</term>,
<note>A form of Christian esotericism and mystical philosophy. It followed the teachings of Martinez de Pasqually and his pupil Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin, emphasizing the "reintegration" of man into his original divine state.</note>

## THE VISIONS OF <term>SWEDENBORG</term>,
<note>Emanuel Swedenborg (1688–1772) was a Swedish scientist and philosopher who claimed to have received revelations from spirits and angels, leading to a unique theological system that fascinated many 18th-century thinkers.</note>

## <term>MAGNETISM</term>, etc.
<note>This refers to "Animal Magnetism" or Mesmerism, a theory popularized by Franz Mesmer. It proposed that an invisible natural fluid flowed through all living things and could be manipulated for healing—often through trances and "magnetic" passes.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut vignette depicting a collection of musical and floral motifs. A round drum or tambourine is centered, draped with garlands of small flowers. Behind the drum are two crossed wind instruments, possibly horns or oboes. Sprigs of leaves and flowers extend outwards from the central arrangement.</image-desc>

## AT AVIGNON.
<note>Avignon was an important site for the publication of "clandestine" or controversial literature. As a Papal territory until 1791, it operated under different censorship laws than the rest of France, making it a hub for esoteric printing.</note>

# OBSERVATIONS
## ON Freemasonry, Martinism, the Visions of Swedenborg, Magnetism, etc.

### I.

THE Freemasons held a National Convention <note>In a Masonic context, a *Convent* refers to a formal legislative assembly of delegates.</note> of the Gauls <note>A Masonic administrative province covering much of France.</note> in Lyon in 1778, and a General Convention in Wilhelmsbad <note>A spa town in Germany; this 1782 meeting was a pivotal moment in Masonic history where the "Strict Observance" rite was largely abandoned.</note> in 1782, in order to introduce a reform into the Masonic Order.

The Respectable Brother <note>Abbreviated as R. F. for *Respectable Frère*.</note> Afascia <note>The Masonic name of Baron von Türkheim, a prominent diplomat and Freemason from Strasbourg.</note>, Prefect of Lorraine, published shortly thereafter a work against the proceedings of this last Convention, titled: *On the General Convention of Freemasons at the waters of Wilhelmsbad, near Hanau.* <note>original Latin: "De Conventu Generali Latomorum apud aquas Wilhelminas, propè Hannauviam." The term *Latomorum* literally means "of stone-cutters," a traditional Latin designation for Freemasons.</note>

Brother Asascia <note>A pseudonym used by a prominent Masonic writer of the period, often associated with the higher degrees or the Illuminati.</note> prides himself on knowing almost all the secrets of the Order, and of having delved into more than twelve different systems which are accepted in several Masonic Provinces; but this Brother, as well as the Lodges of Freemasons who have adopted his work and ordered its printing, prefer the following system.

<margin>*</margin>
They regard the <term>Masonic Order</term> <note>The general term for Freemasonry as it existed in the 18th century.</note> as the continuation of the Order of the Templars itself. Some Templars, having escaped the general proscription <note>The mass arrest and suppression of the Knights Templar by King Philip IV of France in 1307.</note>, preserved and transmitted the signs and other necessary instructions that characterized the different degrees of the Templar Order. These saved Brothers gave it the new name of the "Masonic Order" to avoid the persecutions that the original name, which had become odious, might have provoked.

They were not all initiated into the true and final secret of the Order of the

Brother Afascia <note>A pseudonym likely referring to a contemporary critic or a high-ranking member of a rival Masonic system, possibly associated with the Illuminati or the skeptics of the Templar lineage.</note> maintains that this alleged lineage <note>The "filiation" or direct historical descent of Freemasons from the medieval Knights Templar.</note> cannot worry Governments, because they look upon <term>Masonry</term> as a toy in the hands of a child, who does not break the windows of the house while he plays. It should be observed, however, that there were deputies from the <term>Lodges of Berlin</term> at the general Convention.

The Brothers of Wetzlar <note>original: "Weslard"; likely a misspelling of Wetzlar, a German city significant for its Masonic activity and imperial courts.</note> and of Frankfurt proposed a universal school of <term>eclecticism</term> <note>A Masonic movement that sought to simplify the Order by remaining neutral among competing rites and focusing on shared principles.</note> there through their deputy and their circular letter, in which they expressed themselves thus:

> "Let us choose, in the face of the secular <note>original: "profane"; refers to the world outside of Freemasonry.</note> and Masonic world, neutrality toward all systems known until now—of which none has been or can be proven—meaning: liberty, equality, and independence."

But this plan was rejected in the <term>General Convention</term> <note>The Wilhelmsbad Convention of 1782, a pivotal assembly called to reform the Rite of Strict Observance.</note> for the reason that if eclecticism were the basis of <term>Masonry</term>, it would be necessary to separate the Masons, rather than unite them.

The system of Brother Afascia <note>A pseudonym for a contemporary critic of the Convention's proceedings.</note> was likewise rejected in the said Convention.

Brother *** <note>original: "Le F ***"; the three asterisks are a traditional Masonic abbreviation for "Frère" (Brother) when the name is withheld.</note>, in making his report, says on this subject:

> "Instead of a chimerical system of pride and ostentation regarding the re-establishment of the <term>Order of the Templars</term>, we have returned to the primitive goal of the order. True beneficence, the purest Christian morality, and its direction toward the <term>Supreme Being</term>, the sole source of all good and all happiness; and to making ourselves less unworthy of his love and his grace, by improving ourselves, and working upon these same principles to improve our..."

      The deliberation of December 14, 1783, taken by the prefectural directory <note>The administrative governing body of a specific Masonic district.</note>, in concert with the provincial directory and the priory directory of Lyon, adopts the report made by Brother N***, and the account rendered by Brother Aberemo; it clears him of the suspicions spread against him; it declares that the principles of these two Brothers are those of these directories; this is a point that must not be lost from view when reading this work. It is ordered that this deliberation, this account, and this report shall be printed.

      The extract of this deliberation is signed by Brother *Andreas*, *of the Three Moons* <note>original: "à tribus lunis"; this is a "Latin Knight Name" used in the inner circles of the Rectified Scottish Rite.</note>, Chancellor of the said prefecture; and as, upon his resignation, Brother *Joannes*, *of the Four Poles* <note>original: "à quatuor Polis"; another Latin name indicating a high-ranking member’s symbolic identity.</note>, was appointed Chancellor, it is the latter who signed the circular letter sending the said deliberation.

<meta>This page follows the discussion of the 1783 Lyon directories, moving from the administrative endorsements of Brothers N*** and Aberemo to a philosophical categorization of different types of Freemasons and their varying approaches to "the truth" and the authority of the Grand Master.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<sig>A 5</sig>

## 2.

Freemasons seek the truth that is not known. Most of them are occupied with erecting a political temple <note>In this 18th-century context, a "political temple" refers to a social or civil structure designed to organize society according to Masonic ideals.</note> in its honor, in which they hope to unite all its admirers. Their goal is to offer, in concert, sacrifices worthy of its author. They say the Grand Master <term>Grand Master: the supreme leader of a Masonic Order or jurisdiction</term> of their Order knows it; but they also believe him to be as hidden, as difficult to discover, as this mysterious truth for which he must be the mouthpiece <note>original: "organe"; a medium through which a higher power or truth speaks.</note>.

Some regard it as a secret, as an impenetrable enigma, the answer to which is impossible to find. The Grand Master appears to them as a chimera <note>A figment of the imagination or an unattainable ideal.</note>. However, they believe they must continue their search, but as a form of recreation, and only to anchor the flightiness of the imagination.

Others, regarding all truths as uncertain, want no constraint, and are of the opinion that everyone should be left the freedom to think and act.

<vocab>Freemasons, Grand Master, political temple, mysterious truth, secret, enigma, chimera, freedom of thought, freedom of action</vocab>

<summary>This page categorizes the different philosophical motivations within Freemasonry, describing how various members view the "unknown truth" and the elusive figure of the Grand Master, ranging from sincere seekers to recreational skeptics.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Grand Master, Enlightenment philosophy, Skepticism, Social Order, Symbolic Truth, Masonic structure</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the varying attitudes of Freemasons toward the Grand Master and the "unknown truth," this page explores the lack of earnestness in some seekers and the religious prerequisites for membership within different branches of the Order.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

<insert>24</insert>

Men, generally less reasonable than children when it comes to seeking the truth, do not seem to have any real intention of discovering it. They apply so much levity and so little interest to their search that it remains fruitless. *Always learning, never reaching the knowledge of the truth* <note>original: "Semper discentes nunquam ad veritatem pervenientes," a Latin biblical reference to 2 Timothy 3:7.</note>.

All the Freemasons of whom we have just spoken agree, nevertheless, on the same Author of truth; they only admit into their assemblies those who call themselves Christians. Unlike the Athenians, it is not to the "unknown God" that they have dedicated an altar; yet the Reformed <note>This likely refers to members of the "Rectified" or "Reformed" Masonic rites, such as the Rectified Scottish Rite (RER), which emphasized a return to mystical Christian roots.</note> believe that the proper way to worship Him remains unknown. They seek the true manner of honoring Him and guiding His moral teachings. According to them, He likely taught this to some of His friends through His word, which is now hidden or lost.

There are, however, some Freemasons who seem to believe that it is of no consequence whether one uses one form of worship or another to honor the Divinity, since they admit into their Lodges, without distinction, those who profess the various religions of the world. I cannot personally vouch for this fact, but it is presented as a constant truth.

### 3.

The former <note>This refers to the "Reformed" or "Rectified" Freemasons mentioned on the previous page, who believed the true way to worship God had been lost or hidden.</note> should not feel reassured by their zeal in seeking the only tribute worthy of the true God, if they do not recognize any authority established by Him to define what they must believe about His attributes and His teachings, what they must do to honor and obey Him, and what form of worship <term>culte</term> <note>While "culte" can mean a sect, here it refers to the specific rites and practices used to honor the Divine.</note> He has prescribed for them. They will soon find themselves in agreement only on the name they give Him. Each of them will distort His image so much that there will be at least as much difference between their understandings of this uniform name as there is between the various names others use to describe Him.

Those who give Him the same name will multiply Him by attributing different, and most often incompatible, qualities to Him. They will have among themselves several...

It is true that most of them recognize the necessity of uniting and establishing rules to resolve their uncertainties, and they take steps to carry out this project; yet others sense the impossibility of subjecting all Masons to purely human and arbitrary decisions, which are liable to fluctuate and change in both principle and form. Even if this project could be realized, what would become of the rest of the world—especially the common people <note>The author uses "le peuple" to refer to the uneducated or lower classes, contrasting them with the "initiated" elite of the Lodges.</note>, who comprise its largest part? In places where idolatry reigned, as in the times of the persecutions incited against the Christians, they were forced to reveal the mysteries <term>mysteries: in the early Church, this referred to the "Discipline of the Secret," where specific doctrines and sacraments (like the Eucharist) were hidden from non-believers to prevent misunderstanding or mockery</term> only to those whose zeal and attachment to this divine religion they had sufficiently tested, and who appeared worthy of being admitted to the profession of Christianity. In all places that were not the site of idolatry and persecution, as well as in those where such persecution has ceased, it has always been as easy for the uneducated as for the learned to know all the mysteries of the Christian Religion, the knowledge of which is necessary for salvation. These mysteries have been taught to everyone, and in all times, in the same manner; what was a mystery for some was always a mystery for others as well. The practice of the evangelical counsels <note>In Catholic theology, these are the "counsels of perfection"—poverty, chastity, and obedience—which are encouraged but not mandatory for all believers.</note> had its rules, but they were public, and everyone followed them to the extent that they felt the inclination and disposition to do so.

The authors of these two books, so
highly praised by the <term>Maçons: Freemasons, specifically those involved in the mystical or "illuminist" rites of the 18th century</term>, agree in pro-
scribing the authority of the Catholic Church, & above
all that of its head <note>The author is referring to the Pope</note>, as is easy
to judge by the passages of these books which
are transcribed above. They substitute for
this respectable authority individual reason—

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 34, where he questioned why "enlightened" thinkers of the 18th century—who typically pride themselves on rationalism—are being drawn into secretive, mystical sects.</meta>

even the most well-established natural facts, for fear of appearing to succumb to a childish credulity? If they are reasonable and acting in good faith—as one must charitably assume—they are the dupes of the spirit of error. This spirit succeeds in persuading the men of recent centuries, just as it did those of the first, that they can become Gods. *You shall be as Gods* <note>original: "Eritis sicut Dii"; a reference to the serpent’s temptation of Eve in Genesis 3:5</note>; it seduces them with the hope of obtaining, through the sole power of their desires and efforts, a boundless power and intelligence.

This temptation is more subtle than the one to which the <term>authors of the human race: Adam and Eve, the biblical first parents of humanity</term> succumbed. It was suggested to them that they would know everything they were ignorant of by the virtue of a single fruit; whereas in this modern temptation, it is a matter of seeking <term>science: in this context, "gnosis" or esoteric spiritual knowledge</term> by detaching one's intelligence from everything that is <term>sensible: that which can be perceived by the physical senses</term>, in order to raise it toward intellectual things—the most secret things—and thus penetrate, through the sole force of the will, into the infinite intelligence.

The first temptation had a fruit as a physical sign, whereas the latter is invisible. At their core, they are the same, since in both, it is the will that desires and strives to know that which exceeds the reach of limited intelligences,

<summary>The author compares modern 18th-century mysticism (Illuminism) to the original Fall of Man, arguing that the desire for "infinite intelligence" through willpower is a modern version of the serpent's temptation in Eden.</summary>
<keywords>Original Sin, Enlightenment vs. Mysticism, Willpower, Esoteric Knowledge, Spiritual Pride, Adam and Eve</keywords>

while they are enclosed within a mortal body—and [the spirit of error] seeks to expand the boundaries prescribed to them through means contrary to the will of God, which He makes known through His word.

This Word had given the first humans the command not to eat of the **forbidden fruit**, just as it has since given other commandments to men, in both the **Old** and the **New Testament**. The most essential of these commandments is the one that prescribes obedience to the **Catholic Church**. The <term>spirit of error: a theological term for the devil or a deceptive influence that leads believers away from orthodox truth</term> alone can suggest disobedience; it is the infallible sign that makes this spirit known, whatever <term>sophistry: the use of clever but false arguments, especially with the intention of deceiving</term> it employs, whatever form it assumes—be it by making serpents or the dead speak; whatever miracle it performs, even by transporting those over whom it has power to the highest buildings, upon mountains, or elsewhere, or even into other worlds, and revealing things to them that are impossible for man to know in a natural way <note>The author is likely referring to the Temptation of Christ in the desert, as well as contemporary claims of "astral travel" or spiritualist séances common in 18th-century occult circles.</note>. It succeeds in persuading them that disobedience is a virtue useful to humanity, once it has awakened the desire to satisfy curiosity regarding supernatural things; but it does not—

<page-num>37</page-num>

usually employs this temptation only against those upon whom he has exhausted the pleasures of the mind or the senses; sometimes, however, he brings all his batteries into play at once.

**Revelation**—which is the external word of God, whether written <note>The "written" word refers to the Holy Scriptures or the Bible.</note> or unwritten <note>The "unwritten" word refers to Sacred Tradition, the oral teachings and customs handed down within the Church since the time of the Apostles.</note>—cannot be interpreted by the private thoughts and words of an individual, but rather by that **Church** which He has chosen as His interpreter. *No prophecy of Scripture is made by one's own interpretation; but we have the mind of Christ.* <note>original Latin: "Omnis Prophetia scripturae propria interpretatione non fit ; nos autem sensum Christi habemus." The author is quoting 2 Peter 1:20 and 1 Corinthians 2:16 to argue against individual interpretation of the Bible.</note> It is an act of disobedience to God to undertake this task through one's own <term>lights: an 18th-century term referring to individual human reason or intellectual capacity, often contrasted with divine inspiration</term>, under the false pretext that His word has been lost, and that He has abandoned the human race to the point where there are no longer any legitimate and visible spiritual leaders to make it known and interpret it.

One may think they have the best intentions, following **reason** and seeking to correct the reasoning of others, while in reality being so blinded by pride that they fall into absurdities regarding <term>dogma: the official, authoritative teachings of the Church that believers are required to accept</term> as gross and contrary to reason as those of the **Pagans**. ## 7.

People are misled when they are taught that each of them, by elevating their spirit through the effort of their will, can understand all the reasons of infinite wisdom, and see within the treasures of boundless intelligence everything it has done, and everything it has yet to do for the accomplishment of what these authors call the *great work* <term>Great work: Known in French as the *grand œuvre*. He confused the deceptions of error, which are suited to shake the imagination, with the wonders of the Almighty, which can only give rise to admiration and respect.

It is inconceivable that anyone could make a kind of game out of rattling the imagination using the so-called "marvels" hidden behind the magnetic apparatus. This practice goes so far as to plunge those being magnetized into states of catalepsy <term>Catalepsy: A physical state where a person becomes unresponsive, their muscles grow rigid, and they maintain whatever position they are placed in, often associated with trances or seizures.</term>, sleepwalking <note>original: "somnambules" — here referring to the "magnetic sleep" or induced trance state popularized by Mesmer’s followers.</note>, and convulsions of every kind. Meanwhile, the terrifying effects we have just described inevitably follow; it is impossible for the subjects who submit to this not to feel the consequences sooner or later. One can truly say of this dangerous healing method that the remedy is worse than the disease.

Fortunately, public faith in animal magnetism <term>Animal Magnetism: A theory developed by Franz Mesmer (1734–1815) claiming that an invisible fluid in the body could be manipulated by a "magnetizer" to cure illnesses.</term> is not widespread enough to turn everyone's head. If it were ever possible for it to become that popular, it would have to be banned immediately. This has not been done because of the conviction that its popularity will continue to fade. These excessive outbursts of the imagination are highly contagious <note>original: "épidémiques"</note>. We have seen entire regions fall victim to this kind of psychological epidemic.

The effects cited as being something other than the product of the imagination have not been verified by multiple unbiased witnesses. Furthermore, it can very easily happen that people who believe they can be magnetized will sometimes experience—

...the effects produced by their imagination <term>Imagination: In 18th-century medicine, the "imagination" was considered a powerful force that could cause physical symptoms; the skeptics of magnetism used this to explain away Mesmer's "fluid" as a psychological reaction.</term> at the very moment they are being magnetized, without them even being aware of it; it is well known that they have very often been mistaken. The cures that are achieved through magnetism may simply be the result of ordinary remedies <note>The author suggests that any patient who gets better might actually be responding to standard medical treatments they are taking alongside the "magnetic" sessions.</note> added to the magnetic procedure, or the result of nature <term>Nature: Referring to "vis medicatrix naturae," or the healing power of nature, a belief that the body can often repair itself if left undisturbed.</term>, which would heal even better if its beneficial activity were not so often hindered. No record is kept of those who fall victim to this new method; and it is impossible to know those in whom it has planted, or will plant, the seeds of various diseases and even death. Meanwhile, great care is taken to publish and exaggerate a few chance instances of relief, which are perhaps not even due to magnetism; it has produced very few complete cures.

Many different ailments heal on their own over time without any remedy; but an infinitely greater number of people perish...

But Magnetism, its partisans proclaim, is at least useful for restoring morals by acting upon physical habits. This method is more certain for producing this effect than acting upon moral habits, according to the Author of *Considerations on Magnetism* <note>original: "Considérations sur le Magnétisme"; this refers to a 1784 work by Nicolas Bergasse, who argued that Magnetism could reform society by healing the physical imbalances that led to vice.</note>. He claims that it must be allowed to sleep quietly, and that there is the greatest disadvantage in waking it and making it aware of its own strength. He confuses it with profane love, of which Saint Paul <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul’s exhortations in the New Testament to avoid speaking of or dwelling on carnal or shameful things.</note> forbids us to speak. It is even dangerous to think of it; and one must take great care not to awaken it in hearts; but it is in vain that one would wish to let self-love sleep.

Nothing is more imprudent than to rely on its apparent tranquility. It is never more dangerous than when it seems least so. Continual vigilance is necessary to perceive all its movements, to sniff out its motives and designs, to constantly repair its ravages, and to prevent new ones.

...could the effects of <term>Magnetism: also known as Animal Magnetism or Mesmerism, the theory that an invisible fluid in the body could be manipulated for healing.</term> heal the ills of the soul? Healthy constitutions do not feel its effects, by the admission of its own partisans. They will not be able to say that the health of the body is inseparable from that of the soul, *a healthy mind in a healthy body* <note>original: "mens sana in corpore sano"; a famous aphorism from the Roman poet Juvenal.</note>. This is the wish made throughout the ages by those who sought happiness on earth; but this desire, almost always fruitless, is proof that nothing is rarer.

All temporal goods, such as perfect health, most often form an obstacle to the perfection of the soul. They provide the soul with pleasures from which it is very difficult to detach itself, in order to desire only eternal happiness and to make that the principle of all its movements. It is easier to be reasonable in privation than in enjoyment. *Virtue is made perfect in weakness* <note>original: "Virtus in infirmate perficitur"; a reference to the Bible, 2 Corinthians 12:9, suggesting that physical suffering can lead to spiritual growth.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the regulation of scientific and medical professions, transitioning from the previous page's mention of administrative obstacles to the necessity of professional hierarchy and institutional oversight.</meta>

( 55 )

...and subordination require that the members of every body <note>In the 18th century, a "body" (corps) referred to a professional corporation, guild, or faculty, such as the Faculty of Medicine.</note>, like the subjects of every state, submit themselves to these regulations and to the <term>police: the administrative oversight and regulatory system governing public order and professional conduct.</term>, whether general or specific.

Subordination can lead to despotism; but the despotism of specific professional bodies does not expose one to many dangers. Such instances are prevented or halted by recourse to superior authority. This authority always chooses, to guide its decisions, experts learned in the sciences or in the arts upon which a ruling is to be made; it would run the risk of delivering a false judgment if it deviated from their report, unless that report appeared clearly contrary to the public good; it can have the report repeated in case of suspicion. The opinion of the entire body is usually the most reliable, when care is taken to exclude from the deliberation those members who ought to be <term>recused: disqualified or removed from a decision-making process due to potential bias or conflict of interest.</term>.

The unrestricted liberty of the individual mind leads to all kinds of disorder and can produce no good, not even the good it promises; it tends toward anarchy. It is dangerous to make the public the judge of everything; one should only pass judgment on what one knows, and the enlightened public—that is to say, the <term>experts: "gens de l’art," those specifically trained or experienced in a particular science or craft</term>—are alone competent to decide, each within their own field. It is essential not to strike the imagination and memory—especially of those who possess an **irritable nervous constitution** <term>genre nerveux irritable: An 18th-century medical classification for individuals whose nervous systems were thought to be over-sensitive or prone to excessive reactions.</term>—by the account or the sight of the states just mentioned; the tendency toward **imitation** alone can bring them into being. They imprint traces in the brain and produce movements there which are communicated to the muscles; the latter repeat them, often without the cooperation of the will. In certain subjects, however, the will lends itself to it, when it is determined by the desire to obtain health, applause, or some other advantage. In other subjects, the desire *not* to experience these effects accelerates them, by increasing the movement of the muscles instead of calming it. Acts of **strong passions** <note>In this era, "passions" referred to intense emotional states that were believed to have direct physical effects on the body's health.</note> expressed by certain people are immediately repeated by those who see them.

This **agitation** <note>Referring to the physical tremors or "crises" often seen in Mesmerist therapy sessions.</note> could, by chance, coincide with circumstances favorable to the healing of certain ailments; but it must ordinarily do more...

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Franz Mesmer’s "animal magnetism," specifically analyzing how intense emotional and physical shocks—often induced during Mesmerist sessions—can be more harmful than beneficial to the human "machine" or body.</meta>

( 61 )

...harm than good; what proves this is that fear alone, combined with the danger of perishing in a fire, has claimed many victims, although, among the countless cases of this nature, one or two **paralytics** <note>In the 18th century, this referred to anyone suffering from a loss of motor function or sensation.</note> have been cured by the efforts of the will in order to avoid the consequences of this terror.

**Epilepsy** <note>At the time, "epilepsy" was often a broad diagnosis for various types of seizures or convulsive fits, which were frequently observed during Mesmerist "crises."</note> and several similar ailments have often been produced by the consequences of a shock too strong for the **imagination**, which is then reflected back upon the entire **machine** <term>machine: A common Enlightenment-era philosophical and medical metaphor viewing the human body as a complex mechanical system of nerves, vessels, and organs.</term>. Persist constantly, as if at the **tub** <term>bacquet: The "baquet" was a large wooden vat filled with water and iron filings used in Franz Mesmer’s group therapy sessions; patients held iron rods protruding from it to receive the "magnetic fluid."</term>, in unsettling the imagination for as long as its whims require, without wearying yourself or allowing yourself to be frightened by any of the various effects it is capable of producing; this is what the masters of **Magnetism** <note>Refers to "Animal Magnetism," the precursor to hypnosis, which claimed a subtle fluid connected all living things.</note> recommend to their pupils; the imagination will display alternating bouts of illness, more or less perceptible. Finally settling upon the state that leaves the patients in their least agitated condition, a kind of **well-being** will appear, especially if it is less painful than the situation prior to the first shock caused by Magnetism. All situations of this kind are produced by some similar shock, of which the [origin] is unknown...

<summary>The author argues that while extreme terror or "magnetic" shocks may occasionally cure paralysis through sheer willpower, they more often cause lasting harm like epilepsy by overstimulating the nervous system. He critiques Mesmerist practitioners for intentionally inducing these dangerous "shocks" to achieve a state of exhaustion that the patient mistakes for health.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the social and psychological causes of "nervous" illnesses, transitioning into an argument that the effects of Animal Magnetism are produced entirely by the imagination rather than any physical fluid or metaphysical power.</meta>

( 62 )

...the first cause, and which habit has maintained or increased.

One of the primary causes, undeniably, is the progress of **luxury** <term>luxe: In the 18th century, "luxury" was a major philosophical and medical concern, often blamed for the physical and moral decline of the aristocracy and the burgeoning middle class.</term>, which overstretches the **spring** <term>ressort: Continuing the Enlightenment-era "machine" metaphor, comparing human vitality to the tension in a clock’s spring.</term> of the **passions**, presenting its objects in all possible forms, and causing too extreme alternations of desire, enjoyment, deprivation, and grief for that class of citizens which is almost generally affected by **nervous ailments** <note>maux de nerfs: A broad category of 18th-century illnesses—often called "the vapors"—attributed to the supposedly more sensitive nerves of the wealthy and idle classes.</note>; a part of these would be avoided if there were less distance between fortunes, and more between social ranks.

One sees exactly the same effects produced by diametrically opposed **magnetic systems** <note>This refers to the split between "Fluidists," who followed Mesmer’s idea of a physical magnetic liquid, and "Spiritualists" or "Animists," who believed the power was purely mental or psychological.</note>, since some are founded on a physical action, and others on a supposed metaphysical power. All equally influence the **imagination**, and through it the physical body: nothing demonstrates better that it [the imagination] is the sole cause of all these effects. These systems converge at a point that confirms this conclusion: it is that an initial shock must precede the work to facilitate operations at a certain distance, and make them appear more marvelous. From all this, what can we conclude? That if magnetic operations can be tolerated, it is not...

<summary>The author argues that the "nervous" illnesses of the elite are caused by the excesses of modern luxury and social inequality, and asserts that the effects of Animal Magnetism are purely the result of psychological suggestion (imagination) rather than any real magnetic force.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medical and psychological critique of Animal Magnetism (Mesmerism). It specifically addresses how the treatment manipulates the "fibers" of the brain through the imagination and argues that the violent "crises" or convulsions induced by the practice offer only temporary relief while causing long-term harm to the nervous system.</meta>

( 64 )

their imagination. It is possible, however, to provide them with a kind of relief and a temporary sense of well-being, and to give some rest to the fibers <term>fibers: 18th-century medical theory often described the body as a system of "fibers" that vibrated or held tension; mental health was thought to depend on the physical state of these fibers in the brain.</term> where the traces of ideas—the source of their ills—are located, by stimulating other fibers where the traces of some other vivid emotion <note>original: "affection vive". In the medical language of the time, an "affection" was a physical or mental state produced by an external influence.</note> reside, and by bringing that emotion to life. One can strike them forcefully, for example, with the hope of recovery, while their greatest ailment is the fear of the contrary; but ordinary physicians can produce this effect without exposing patients to further shocks <note>original: "ébranlements". This refers to the physical and nervous jolts caused by the magnetic treatment.</note> and to convulsions. All magnetic crises <term>crises: The violent physical reactions—including screaming, sobbing, or involuntary jerking—that Mesmer claimed were the "magnetic fluid" breaking through internal blockages to heal the patient.</term> usually provide only a passing relief, and often cast the subjects into other ailments that are more dangerous in their consequences, even though they may appear less so.

The method indicated by nature to cure nervous ailments is to avoid everything that can strike the imagination, to abandon for a long time all occupation and mental strain <note>original: "contention d'esprit". This refers to intense intellectual focus or worry, which was believed to over-tax the nervous system.</note>, and to devote oneself solely, or as much as possible, to exercise: one never does enough of it; thus, one does not receive the natural effect it should produce. To turn away from this method and substitute Magnetism in its place is to cast the subjects into states that are not natu-

...contradictions <note>The OCR text begins with "dictions," which is the conclusion of the word "contradictions" from the preceding page.</note> of his works. The vast knowledge and orderly life of Swedenborg appeared to the spirit of darkness <note>original: "l'esprit des ténèbres." In this context, it refers to a demonic influence intended to deceive the faithful.</note> as a means to give weight to his errors and to lead many to adopt them. All evil thoughts come from this spirit, just as all good ones come from God; thus, even if this author had experienced nothing that was beyond his natural faculties, his works are nonetheless the fruit of the spirit of error.

# 69

He is depicted there as full of probity <note>original: "probité." In this context, it refers to a high standard of moral integrity and honesty.</note>, sincere, loving God and men. However, the love of God cannot be reconciled with disobedience to His will, which is clearly characterized in the writings and the beliefs of this author; but there are two loves of God: one is supernatural, which He alone can give, and which is founded upon the truth, and consequently upon the faith of the Catholic Church.

Without this faith, there is no true love of God, no love of neighbor or of oneself that is useful for salvation <note>original: "salut." This refers to the deliverance from sin and its consequences, believed by Christians to be brought about by faith in Christ.</note>. These must necessarily be conformed to the word of God, through which He gives the knowledge of the truth and of His will, so that it might be possible to fulfill it. One cannot believe and persistently teach until death errors contrary to the word and the will of God, and still possess His love—that is to say, charity <note>original: "charité." In theological terms, this is "caritas," the supernatural virtue by which we love God above all things and our neighbor for His sake.</note>, which makes one pleasing in His eyes and which justifies.

The theological process by which a sinner is made righteous or "just" before God.</note> is faith in God, the Rewarder <note>original: "Rémunérateur." A traditional theological term for God as the ultimate judge who rewards the good and punishes the wicked.</note>. One does not, therefore, love His real perfections, since one does not know Him and does not have the faith of the Catholic Church, through which alone they can be known; and faith itself is not enough without works...

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on the psychological and spiritual nuances of divine love, following a critique of Swedenborg’s visions. <page-num>75</page-num>
<sig>D 2</sig>

case a refinement of <term>self-love</term> <note>original: "amour-propre." In 18th-century thought, this refers to a prideful, narcissistic form of self-interest, as opposed to "amour de soi," which is a natural instinct for self-preservation.</note>; it provides a taste of a sublime and delicate pleasure, which far surpasses that of the false love of God, just as the latter is superior to all other pleasures. This latter has no foundation other than error and <term>concupiscence</term> <note>original: "concupiscence." A theological term for the human inclination toward worldly or carnal desires rather than the divine.</note> or self-love; the former is always founded on truth, on the renunciation of one's own judgment; it is found only in those who profess the Catholic faith; and its very excesses become excusable, because, although a little self-love is almost always mixed in, and although we seek pleasure therein, it is in the true God; an advantage which is found only in the Catholic Church.

One can surrender oneself without danger to **effective love**; it leads one to constantly do everything that pleases this Supreme Being, to avoid everything that displeases Him, and to manage one's health—not as one wishes, but in the manner He prescribes—in order to be able to devote oneself, for as long as He deems appropriate, to the exercise of this love; it is as pleasing to Him as it is useful to humanity. ( 88 )

[God] is the only spouse of all souls and of all spirits who are in Heaven, because they will only be able to unite themselves to Him through the pleasure they take in His own [pleasure], and in His infinite perfections. Mr. Nicole <note>Pierre Nicole (1625–1695) was a prominent French Jansenist theologian and author. He is cited here to provide a traditional counter-argument to Swedenborg’s idea of a social Heaven; Nicole argues that spirits in Heaven are self-sufficient in God.</note> says that in the world of spirits, there is no longer any true society, because they no longer have needs, nor any dependence upon one another. In whatever number they may be, they are in a state of complete separation. The happiness of each constitutes that of the others. They love one another with a perfect love; but this union or spiritual society of pleasures and wills does not interrupt their solitude. This same Author says elsewhere: one must not deceive oneself, nor extend concupiscence <term>concupiscence: the theological term for human desire for worldly or carnal things, which traditionalists believe must be entirely overcome to reach God.</term> as far as Heaven, by desiring there the enjoyment of the goods of concupiscence. God will be the sole portion of the elect. He will be their only bliss. Their unique joy will be to see Him, to love Him, to be...

subjected; to see that He reigns fully over them; to have nothing in them that opposes His justice. This is what will form the basis of their happiness. They will consider everything else only in relation to this essential good.

Saint Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD) was a foundational philosopher and theologian whose writings shaped much of Western Christianity. He is cited here to provide historical weight to the argument that Heaven is spiritual rather than physical.</note> thinks the same. Let us be careful, he says, not to promise ourselves pleasures in Heaven similar to those we enjoy on earth.... Let us banish these low and carnal thoughts from our minds. Let us prepare ourselves for something ineffable <term>ineffable: something too great or sacred to be expressed or described in words.</term>. Let us purify our hearts from all earthly and secular affections. We will see an object that will make us happy, and this single object will suffice for us. We will be filled with the goods of your house; but what are the goods of this house? Shall we imagine a magnificent palace full of all sorts of riches, etc.? These things belong to Babylon <note>In theological writing, "Babylon" often represents the material world, vanity, and the confusion of earthly desires, standing in opposition to the City of God.</note>; the good of the house of God is not of this nature. We will be filled, says the Prophet, with the goods of your house; your temple is holy; it is admirable in justice. These are the goods of this house.

Swedenborg, on the contrary, makes it a dwelling that resembles that of the earth. He places palaces there similar to those of

By straying from the interpretation that Jesus Christ gives through His Church of the truths He revealed, one begins by weakening them and ends by annihilating them; and as these truths are the support of religion and morality, to undermine their foundation is to destroy religion and morality themselves. It is above all in this manner that I accuse the spirit who dictated the works of <term>Swedenborg: Emanuel Swedenborg (1688–1772), a Swedish scientist-turned-mystic who claimed to have conversed with angels and visited the afterlife.</term> of having this destruction as its primary goal.

It is a crude and dangerous trap to admit the existence of bodies whose use is very material for all bodily functions, yet becomes spiritual only for marriage. Swedenborg, who makes this subtle distinction, seems to have felt its lack of plausibility, since he claims that the angels are always clothed in the lower heavens, and that he has left naked only those of the highest heaven. [having] advanced that he is always grand, always elevated, always sublime in everything he proposes, and that the unity of his principles is never contradicted nor belied in all his various works: he contradicts himself by asserting that all spirits were once men, while also agreeing that every man has beside him and within him a good and a bad spirit, belonging to the world of spirits, the first of whom suggests good to him, and the other evil. ...all pleasures, to make them find a greater one in what He commands them than in what He forbids them, or at least to give them the strength to prefer His pleasure to all others.

He has made His Church the depository of this strength; He attaches it to what she prescribes to be believed and practiced. Although her decisions appear powerless by themselves; although it is impossible to understand how those she gives regarding dogma <term>Dogma: The core principles or established beliefs of the Church, held to be divinely revealed and authoritative, often contrasted here with human "reason."</term> are in conformity with reason, it is not reasonable to refuse to submit to them, since that is to refuse to obey God Himself. He whose thoughts and wills <note>original: "volontés"</note> are perfectly conformed to those of God, which are announced to him by the Church, sincerely adopts all her decisions instead of fighting them. He puts into use all the means she prescribes, as those to which divine grace <term>Divine Grace: In Catholic theology, a supernatural gift from God bestowed upon human beings for their salvation and sanctification.</term> is attached. He undoubtedly receives much illumination <note>original: "lumieres"; literally "lights," referring here to spiritual insight or clarity of mind granted by God.</note> and strength, but for the purpose of knowing his passions, fighting them, and conquering them—and not for having revelations or performing miracles. He believes these are reserved, by infinite wisdom, for the pressing needs of the Church, in order to preserve it and not to destroy it.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 105, which stated that miracles and revelations are rare gifts reserved for the specific needs of the Church. <page-num>106</page-num>

He knows that their manifestation, if too frequent, would diminish their miraculous nature <note>original: "le merveilleux"; referring to the sense of wonder or the supernatural quality of miracles.</note> and their fruit.

He is far from thinking that it is enough for him to wish for something in order to bring about what he wants, even the greatest of wonders. He knows that his will cannot be carried out in matters contrary to the laws of nature, except when it conforms to the will of God. He follows the instruction that Jesus Christ gave against this kind of temptation. One must not throw oneself from the top of a high tower with the intent of coming to no harm, in the foolish persuasion that the Creator always carries out the will of His creature because He watches over its preservation. Jesus Christ forbids it, because that would be to "tempt God." It is to act, for the same reason, against this prohibition to imagine one can entirely lift the veil covering the mysteries of faith; to know clearly all supernatural truths through natural light <note>original: "lumieres naturelles"; this refers to human reason and logic unaided by divine intervention.</note> alone, without the help of revelation <term>Revelation: The communication of truth from God to humanity, which the author argues cannot be replaced by human logic.</term>, and even by rejecting its decisions and its authority. To do so is to believe oneself the repository of the omnipotence <term>Omnipotence: The quality of having unlimited or very great power; here, the "all-powerfulness" of God.</term> of God, and to overturn the order He has established—doing so against His will and without His participation.

It is not for us to prescribe to Him the time and the circumstances in which miracles are necessary; He alone knows them. Clothed in the strength of the <term>Almighty</term><note>A title for God emphasizing His total power over all creation.</note>, directed by his infallible wisdom, you will taste nothing but his good pleasure; all others will seem insipid to you and will be indifferent to you; in whatever situation you may be, certain that he has permitted or ordered it, and that he wants you to be content—whether to remain there or to leave, if he deems it appropriate—you will submit to one or the other with true <term>resignation</term><note>A spiritual state of humble submission to the divine will, regardless of personal desire or circumstance.</note>. You will not serve him otherwise than he prescribes; you will seek only his pleasure in those things that he provides for you. Tranquil, because he wills it, even in the height of storms, you will no longer appear led by a will of your own, but by his.

When the presence of pain or the absence of pleasure gives rise within you to the desire to see this state of being changed—just as **Jesus Christ** experienced for your instruction in the <term>Garden of Olives</term><note>Also known as Gethsemane, the garden where Jesus prayed and accepted his fate before his arrest, demonstrating the ultimate submission of human will to divine will.</note>—it must always be under the condition that this change conforms to **His will**. As soon as His will is known to you, you will be content, like Jesus, that the change does not take place; and you will endure, following His example, pain or the loss of pleasure with the firmness, composure, and tranquility that He has the right to expect from a courageous and faithful servant.

This courage and this strength are not, you say, found in nature; but the history of nations provides us with more than one example of them. These examples were produced only by human perspectives, by the love of an <term>ephemeral glory</term><note>Fleeting or temporary fame and honor, as opposed to eternal spiritual rewards.</note>, or even by motives that were supernatural in appearance but were actually the effect of certain natural and secret motives. It is true that man rarely does painful things precisely because they please **God**, whereas he constantly does even more painful things to please himself and to please the world, preferring error to **reason**.

The folds of our **heart** are infinite; it is impossible for us to sound them perfectly. We often contradict ourselves and yield to pleasure, especially in circumstances where we believe we will not be seen.

Our desires are moved by the motive of doing good, of avoiding evil, and of pleasing **God**, only when we recognize our **powerlessness** and desire that He forms within us, and through us, these good desires and the actions that conform to them. These actions cannot be good in the eyes of God when we attribute to ourselves that which is good in them, and when we do not return all the **glory** to Him.

This refers to the influential work "Des erreurs et de la vérité" (1775) by Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin, a famous French mystic and philosopher.</note> proves the necessity of a single form of worship led by a single head; he announces that he knows the true one, and refuses to name it, under the vain pretext that he does not want to judge established religions, and that it is not one man who should reveal it to another man, but the **intelligent cause** <term>intelligent cause: original "cause intelligente." It must therefore be entrusted to a body that is always subsisting, whose members are constantly replaced. There is no union without a point of meeting, no body without a head. A body must have a sign of indication and a rallying point by which it can be recognized. ## 12.

No body on earth is invested with an authority that unites these two characteristics <note>Referring to the permanence and the divine origin mentioned on the previous page.</note>, except for the Catholic Church. It is of divine institution, founded by Jesus Christ, following the fulfillment of the prophecies, which have been renewed from age to age since the beginning of the world. Its founder is recognized as the Messiah, the Word <note>original: "le Verbe." In Christian theology, this refers to the *Logos*, the eternal Son of God through whom all things were created.</note>, the Envoy of God, by all Christians—of whatever sect they may be—and even by the Muhammadans <note>original: "Mahométans." This is a historical term used in the 18th century to refer to Muslims.</note>. These titles are given to him along with those of Prophet and Apostle in several chapters of the Quran <note>original: "l'Alcoran."</note>. Almost all the peoples of the world profess Christianity or Muhammadanism <note>Islam</note>; thus the authority of Jesus Christ is the greatest it is possible to employ on earth. It is that of the Envoy of God, and that of God himself who invested him with it.

<summary>The author identifies the Catholic Church as the only institution possessing both divine origin and universal recognition, arguing that even the Quran acknowledges the high status of Jesus Christ as a prophet and messenger.</summary>
<keywords>Catholic Church, Jesus Christ, authority, Messiah, Word, Muhammadans, Quran, Christianity, Islam, divine institution</keywords>

His titles have been sufficient to make him recognized by all nations; they agree in condemning the obstinacy of the Jews <note>The author uses "blindness" and "obstability" in a historical theological sense, common in 18th-century apologetics, to describe the Jewish rejection of Jesus as the Messiah.</note>, whose blindness does not allow them to see what is evident to the entire universe: that all the characteristics of the Messiah, as traced in their books, apply to Jesus Christ. To be certain, even in a natural way, of what he said without being mistaken, one must go back to the times in which he lived through the help of an uninterrupted tradition; this tradition must unite the beliefs of Christians from all places and all times, so as to avoid the errors that private prejudices—belonging to a particular nation or century—might have introduced; this is the first rule we have established, and it is one that can encounter no difficulty.

The tradition that bears this character is that of the Catholic Church; all others have clearly departed from the tradition received in their own century, under the pretext of introducing changes and reforms, assuming that errors had crept in—something God could not have permitted after his promise. <meta>This page completes the thought from the previous page regarding the motives of religious sects. It then transitions into a core argument for the necessity of the Catholic Church as a stabilizing authority for both the learned and the uneducated.</meta>

the truth of their opinions. They are very indifferent to them in all cases where they are not useful for the preservation and defense of their interests or their pleasures.

Since the masses <note>original: "le grand nombre," referring to the uneducated majority or the general public.</note> are incapable of interpreting the word of Jesus Christ, it is indispensable to resort to authority; it must captivate the learned and the ignorant alike. Now, among all the peoples of the world, one would seek in vain for an authority respected in all places and in all centuries as much as the Catholic Church. Considering it even from a purely human standpoint, it is therefore the only one capable of directing and subduing human reason; otherwise, Providence would have given all men the most ardent desire to know the truth, yet would have deprived them of all means to do so—or rather, they would deprive themselves of these means by refusing to unite and agree upon the choice of a visible authority. The Catholic Church has undoubtedly lost some of its authority among several nations; but it still preserves many zealous and virtuous supporters in their midst; furthermore, it has conquered several new nations, and its authority is still found to be infinitely more considera- <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing as "considérable" (considerable) on the following page.</note>

He has promised to prevent the Church from teaching error in its public decrees, and nothing is easier for Him, since He is the master of events. He is also the master of souls and hearts, and can compel even those who do not love the truth to speak in conformity with it—even if they are moved to speak thus only by human motives <note>original: "motifs humains," referring to worldly, political, or social reasons rather than spiritual conviction</note>.

If it were possible for the Church to teach error, God could not hold it against those who believe what she teaches them. I repeat this point: even the enemies of the Church adopt this assertion. Meanwhile, Catholics are convinced that those who do not submit to the Church's decisions are at fault and cannot be saved. This obedience is therefore the safest path, and consequently the one that prudence—guided by Divine reason—commands us to follow. What a terrible account must be rendered by those who lead men away from this path by stripping away, or even merely weakening, the confidence they should have in the only authority whose decisions can justify them in the eyes of God.

This system is even more inconsistent than that of the first group <note>Referring to the "Illuminists" like Swedenborg mentioned on the previous page, who believe God must speak directly to every individual.</note>; for if the former negate the goodness of **God** by denying that he has sufficiently provided for the instruction of the multitude, the latter <note>Referring to those (often early Protestant or sectarian thinkers) who argued that the true Church existed for only three centuries before becoming corrupted or "invisible."</note> reject at once both his power and his goodness by asserting that he only provided for it for three centuries. If he judged it necessary during that time, he acknowledged its perpetual necessity; he therefore lacked either the power or the goodness to sustain it, if he did not do so.

## 14.

The peace of the world depends on the greater or lesser degree of authority that resides not only in temporal <gloss>secular or governmental</gloss> and physical power, but also in spiritual and moral power. ## 15.

We must not confuse the decisions
of the Church on dogma <note>In this context, "dogma" refers to the core, unchangeable truths of the Christian faith as defined by the Church.</note> and morality with
its decisions relating to discipline <note> "Discipline" refers to the practical rules and regulations of the Church (such as fasting or the celibacy of the clergy) which, unlike dogma, can be modified over time.</note>. Sup-
pose that one had noticed reforms to
be made in discipline, and that the Pastors
of the Church had refused to comply;
should the Reformers <note>Referring to the leaders of the Protestant Reformation, such as Luther or Calvin.</note> have done to themselves and
their followers an irreparable and in-
finite harm, by assuming the impossible—that the Church
had fallen into error on points
of dogma and morality? It is for
having failed to make this judicious distinction
that most of the enemies of the Church have
caused its misfortunes and its losses. They
led Sovereigns to believe that in
order to escape its discipline,
and not allow themselves to be dominated by it in this...

The <term>Parlements</term> <note>In pre-revolutionary France, Parlements were high courts of justice rather than legislative bodies; they played a key role in regulating both civil and religious life.</note> do not permit, within the Catholic Church and within the state, any other assemblies relating to religion than the public assemblies of the members of any given Church, held under the eyes and the authority of the Pastor <note>In this context, "Pastor" refers to the local ecclesiastical head, such as a bishop or parish priest, responsible for the spiritual guidance of the community.</note>, who is its head.

Various printed works are sufficient to instruct mankind. ## 16.

**What** the Apostles <term>Apostles: The primary disciples of Jesus Christ who, according to Christian tradition, were sent out to preach the Gospel and establish the Church.</term> taught—having learned it from Jesus Christ—is what the Church teaches today. Its decisions, throughout the various centuries, have served only to develop and put into writing this venerable tradition whenever certain Heretics <term>Heretics: Individuals whose religious beliefs or opinions contradict the established doctrines of the Church.</term> have attempted to contradict or reform it. It is necessary that nothing within it be changed, both to place a check on human inconstancy and for the instruction of the inhabitants of the earth, even those who do not adopt it in its entirety. The Church is a bright and inextinguishable torch, in whose light the moral errors of other religions are made visible; this would cover them in confusion if those errors were blatant, and it prevents them from straying too far, in this regard, from the light with which the Catholic Church illuminates reason. It is to her that other religions owe the obligation of not having fallen back into the errors and excessive follies of Paganism <note>Original: "Paganiſme" — referring to the polytheistic and non-Christian religious practices of the ancient world, which the author views as irrational.</note>; one can judge to

Although they feel remorse after having acted against their conscience and reason, it is rare that they consult it <note>Referring back to the "reason" or "conscience" mentioned on the previous page.</note> before acting with all the attention necessary to distinguish it from all the apparent lights, more or less false, that dazzle them. This attention costs effort; it requires a painful and continual labor, through which the soul constantly resists all the attractions that are not those of reason and truth. This resistance is all the more difficult because the former <note>The "attractions" mentioned previously, which appeal to the senses rather than the mind.</note> are much more sensible <term>Sensible: In 18th-century philosophy, this refers to things perceived through the physical senses or immediate emotions, which were often seen as distractions from higher rational thought.</term>, and have more seductive appearances. A long habit of reflection, sustained by an ardent desire not to be mistaken; an excessive distrust of one's own judgment, much prudence to discern that of others before adopting it, and a total and continual renunciation of what pleases, are absolutely necessary to distinguish the infinite and convenient roads of error from the narrow paths of truth <note>A common philosophical and religious metaphor (referencing Matthew 7:13-14) suggesting that the "wrong" path is easy and popular, while the "right" path is difficult and requires discipline.</note>. It is even more difficult to walk in them and to follow them constantly without ever straying. No moment of rest is to be found there; it always becomes dangerous or fatal. It is only possible to avoid its pitfalls through a labor that begins...

<summary>This page describes the rigorous mental and moral discipline required to distinguish truth from error, arguing that the path of reason is a constant, difficult labor against seductive sensory attractions.</summary>

...with life, and which can only end with it.

It is by this arduous means that the human spirit can procure its nourishment: the knowledge of truth. *In the sweat of your face you shall eat bread; man does not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God.* <note>original: "In sudore vultus tui vesceris pane ; non in solo pane vivit homo sed in omnis verbo , quod procedit de ore Dei." This is a composite of two Biblical passages: Genesis 3:19, regarding the labor imposed after the Fall, and Matthew 4:4, regarding spiritual nourishment.</note> This labor is a penance to which all men are condemned; it is contrary to the nature of spirits <note>In this context, "spirits" refers to the human mind or soul as an intellectual being.</note>; they desire to enjoy, without effort, the pleasure of seeing and following the truth, and they have a perpetual tendency toward the pleasure of rest, which distances them from truth even when they believe they are seeking it. Eternal labor is the punishment for temporal rest, just as eternal rest is the reward for temporal labor <note>A philosophical play on the concept of "rest." The author argues that intellectual or spiritual laziness in this life (temporal rest) leads to eternal struggle, while disciplined effort (temporal labor) leads to the "eternal rest" of heaven.</note>. They would exhaust themselves in superfluous efforts if God did not provide them a means of finding the truth by making His will known to them. This will is always in conformity with His reason, which is His word, since He does not speak—and cannot speak—except as it pleases Him, that is to say, as He wills. His almighty will cannot be contrary to the truth, because He knows it perfectly, and truth naturally pleases when it is evident. It is impossible that He would have caused error to be taught by His Prophets,

They are skeptical about the possibility of curing certain <term>diseases of the soul: vices, passions, or spiritual failings that lead one away from God</term>, because these diseases please them; on the contrary, they are excessively credulous about the possibility of curing those of the body, because those are displeasing to them. Thus, they very easily become the dupes of every new method proposed for curing the latter.

These different diseases lead to death: the former to that of the soul, the latter to that of the body—which is dead when it is without <term>motion: "mouvement," referring here to the vital activity or "animal spirits" believed to animate the living body</term>. To preserve the life of the body as much as possible—its union with the <term>principle of its life: the animating force or soul that provides vitality to physical matter</term>—it is necessary to moderate motion when it is too strong, and to increase it when it is too weakened throughout the whole machine <note>The "machine" was a common 18th-century metaphor for the human body, viewed as a complex system of gears, fluids, and levers</note>, or only in some part, due to the effect of some physical or <term>moral cause: "cause morale" refers to psychological, emotional, or spiritual influences rather than physical ones</term>.

The soul is dead when it is separated from the principle of its happiness <note>referring to God, the source of all true joy in this theological framework</note>. It does not feel its death while it is united to the body, because it is never entirely deprived of pleasant sensations.

## 17.

Many nations regard the discipline of the Church as contrary to their temporal <term>temporal: relating to worldly, secular, or civil life as opposed to spiritual or eternal matters</term> interests and their politics; but are they sufficiently free from prejudice? Do they possess insights <note>The author uses "lumieres" (lights), a common Enlightenment-era term for intellectual clarity or reason.</note> vast enough to judge soundly what is useful or harmful to their well-being, even in temporal matters? The plan of deviating in no way from the tradition universally adopted since Jesus Christ <note>original: "Jefus-Chrift"</note> invincibly opposes variations; and seventeen centuries of experience provide a perfect demonstration, considering this fact with reason alone. The order of the universe proclaims an infinite intelligence; Swedenborg agrees on this; we must therefore regard as false all reasoning that tends to destroy it. Why multiply the worlds under the pretext that a single one does not seem vast enough to provide a sufficient idea of the power of God? Must we not eventually put an end to the vain curiosity of men, and to this astonishing multitude of worlds, after having increased their number beyond the limits of human intelligence? Infinite power is not increased by billions of worlds, nor diminished by a single one, since they are all but a point compared to the immensity of God.

love and the pleasure he has from having given so many proofs of this love. *He was offered because he himself willed it* <note>original: "*Oblatus est quia ipse voluit*," a Latin phrase from the Book of Isaiah (53:7) often cited in Catholic liturgy to emphasize that Jesus chose to suffer for humanity's sake.</note>. His father only consented to see him suffer so much because he knew how much his <term>Passion|The period of intense suffering undergone by Jesus Christ between the Last Supper and his death on the Cross.</term> would serve his happiness by increasing the number of the <term>blessed|In this context, the "bienheureux" refers to the souls who have achieved salvation and reside in the presence of God.</term>. It is for the same reason that he sends crosses <note>The author uses "crosses" as a metaphor for the personal trials, illnesses, or hardships that a person must bear during their life.</note> to his <term>elect|Those individuals chosen or predestined by God for eternal life in heaven.</term>. They will be happy in proportion to their love and the proofs they will have given of it through suffering: in such a way that God draws the eternal happiness of his Son and his elect from their temporal evils <note>In theological writing, "temporal" refers to things that are temporary and belong to this physical world, as opposed to "eternal" things.</note>.

God is perfectly happy, although there are <term>reprobates|Those who are rejected by God and consigned to eternal damnation; the opposite of the elect.</term>; but he does not look with pleasure upon their eternal misfortune; they do not serve God’s happiness; whereas he takes pleasure in what he has done, through each of his elect, to lead them to happiness, and in the free consent he has caused them to form. They please him—or rather, he takes pleasure from all eternity in his own power and intelligence, by which he has rendered them pleasing in his eyes; this pleasure is part of his happiness. They have the advantage of having contributed to it, of having been the instruments of it, and they rejoice in it, as well as in the pleasure he takes in the other elect.

The respectable Mother of Jesus is she whose joy is the most perfect. It is surprising that the Christian sects <note>The author likely refers to various Protestant groups who, during this period, were often criticized by Catholic writers for reducing the liturgical and devotional importance of the Virgin Mary.</note>, while honoring the Son, have so little veneration for the Mother. Yet what could be more fitting to inspire it than the choice of the Almighty, and the pleasure or the will he had to clothe her with this glorious quality! He has so directed events and circumstances that he made her a privileged creature, enlightened enough to feel vividly the full extent of her happiness, and humble enough to attribute none of it to herself. She acknowledged her debt <note>The original French uses "devoir," which can mean duty, but in this context of gratitude, it implies acknowledging what she owed to God’s grace.</note> solely to the power and mercy of God, and above all to his promises regarding the <term>Messiah|The "Anointed One" or Savior promised in the Hebrew Bible, whom Christians identify as Jesus.</term>. This is the opening line of the hymn of praise uttered by Mary in the Gospel of Luke (1:46–55) after the Annunciation.</note>.

O You, who work toward the vain reform of the **Masonic Order** <term>Masonic Order|Freemasonry. In the 18th century, several Masonic branches, particularly in France and Germany, sought spiritual or mystical "reforms" to reconnect the movement with religious traditions.</term>, reflect on the usefulness of the plan I propose, and on the danger and the illusion of any other. No one is better suited than you to produce this happy revolution, since your zeal and your talents have been sufficient to set in motion and unite a great number of enlightened people, in an **age of enlightenment** <note>original: "siecle de lumieres". A reference to the 18th-century intellectual movement that championed reason, though here the author applies it to a spiritual context.</note>, and in that part of the globe where they shine the most. What solid glory and what satisfaction for you, what happiness for your brothers! Instead of attacking the **Church**, its perpetuity, and its visibility, turn all your weapons against those of its members whose conduct is not in accordance with its precepts. If it has subsisted at all times, despite their corruption, how great will be its beauty once you have forced them toward a reform as useful for them as for the rest of the world!

It would be to delude yourselves, or to wish to deceive others <note>original: "vouloir en imposer". Mr. **Druck** <note>Friedrich Wilhelm von Druck (1754–1807), a German historian and professor at the Karlsschule in Stuttgart.</note>, Professor of History, delivered a Discourse in **Stuttgart** <note>original: "Stutgard"</note>, on the birthday of the **Duke of Württemberg** <note>original: "Duc de Wirtemberg", likely referring to Charles Eugene.</note>, concerning the wanderings of the human spirit in two different eras. # T A B L E O F C O N T E N T S

ACCOUNT *of what occurred regarding the reform of* <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>original: "Franc-Maçonnerie". A fraternal organization that uses the tools and language of stonemasonry as metaphors for moral and spiritual development.</note>*,* page 1

*Freemasons are unaware of the true secret of their Order, which they believe is known only to a hidden* <term>Grand Master</term> <note>original: "Grand-Maître". The highest-ranking official within a Masonic jurisdiction.</note>*. This secret must contain the plan of the spiritual edifice, which the* <term>Great Architect of the Universe</term> <note>original: "grand Architecte de l'univers". A deistic or non-denominational term used in Masonry to represent the Supreme Being or God.</note> *causes to be built, to unite all intelligent beings, and to make them happy,* 9

*The reformed Freemasons are in error, in seeking to discover, by their own private enlightenment, the plan and the details of this edifice, and in undertaking to conduct it, according to the principles of the reform, without wishing to refer to those whom the Great Architect has made the guardians of this plan, and to whom he has given the necessary authority to direct its execution,* 11

<summary>This page contains the start of the Table of Contents, outlining an account of Masonic reform and asserting that many Masons are mistaken about the Order's true secrets and divine authority.</summary>

# T A B L E O F C O N T E N T S

*A part of the system of their reform is contained, by their own admission, in the book* <term>Of Errors and of Truth</term> <note>original: "Des Erreurs et de la Vérité" (1775). *The authors of these two books add to the error of the* <term>reformed Freemasons</term> <note>Refers to Masonic branches that sought to return to mystical or occult origins during the 18th century.</note> *that of saying that everyone can become a prophet and an inspired being. They renew the sect of the* <term>Illuminati</term> <note>original: "Illuminés". # T A B L E O F C O N T E N T S

<term>Swedenborg</term><note>Emanuel Swedenborg (1688–1772), a Swedish scientist and mystic who claimed to have frequent visions of the spirit world. His writings heavily influenced several high-degree Masonic rites in the late 18th century.</note>, by following the same principles, imagined himself to have received revelations, and to have made a journey to **Heaven** and **Hell**. A description of what he wrote. He traced a new plan filled with errors, 78

Errors and excesses to which all those who, like Swedenborg, give themselves over to their <term>private spirit</term><note>original: "esprit particulier." This refers to the practice of following one's own subjective spiritual intuition rather than established religious or institutional doctrine.</note> are exposed. These are avoided by submission to the authority established by the **Grand Architect** <note>original: "grand Architecte." In a Masonic context, this refers to the "Grand Architect of the Universe," a non-denominational term for God.</note>. It is impossible for every **Mason** to know, by himself, so vast a plan, and to work toward its execution without knowing what his task is, and without being directed, 99

By refusing to take the advice of those who are the keepers of the plan and its details, the **Freemasons** have lost the knowledge of it. They seek it through deep and subtle reflections; they admit that the multitude is not capable of such things, and yet maintain that they must perform them, though without expecting any result. The necessity of acknowledging an authority: the characteristics it must possess, 125

<summary>This page of the Table of Contents critiques Emanuel Swedenborg's mystical revelations and warns against the "private spirit" in Freemasonry. It argues that without submission to a central authority and a guided "plan," Masons lose the true knowledge of their craft.</summary>

<keywords>Emanuel Swedenborg, Heaven and Hell, Grand Architect, Masonic authority, private spirit, spiritual errors, Masonic plan</keywords>
